Micromobility in New York City - Regulation, Accountability, and Safety

Committee on Transportation and Infrastructure | Committee on Consumer and Worker Protection | Committee on Public Safety

Chairs: Shaun Abreu, Harvey Epstein, Oswald J. Feliz
September 30, 2026 · 10hrs 1m · Watch on councilnyc.viebit.com ↗ · View on council.nyc.gov ↗

Meeting overview

The NYC Council's Committee on Transportation and Infrastructure, jointly with the Committees on Consumer and Worker Protection and Public Safety, held an extended oversight hearing on micromobility regulation, safety, and accountability in New York City. The hearing was prompted in part by a spike in e-bike fatalities — cyclist deaths have doubled year-over-year to 20 in 2026 — and by the death of 17-year-old Gabriel Mercado just outside City Hall. Twenty-three bills and two resolutions were under consideration, covering everything from Class 3 e-bike bans and dynamometer testing to delivery app licensing, liability insurance, and algorithmic accountability for delivery platforms. The scale and ambition of the package reflects genuine public pressure, but the hearing also revealed that many laws already on the books — including a 2013 requirement that delivery workers wear identifying vests — have gone unenforced for years. Several council members and witnesses made the reasonable observation that passing twenty-three new laws may matter less than enforcing the ones that already exist.

A central and genuinely unresolved tension ran through the entire hearing: whether the primary problem is reckless individual riders or systemic corporate pressure from delivery apps that algorithmically coerce workers into unsafe speeds under threat of deactivation. Delivery workers and their advocates (Workers Justice Project, Los Deliveristas Unidos, NDLON) argued forcefully that app companies — DoorDash, Uber Eats, Grubhub — set impossible delivery windows and punish lateness, making unsafe riding a condition of employment rather than a personal choice. DCWP Commissioner Levin announced a $131.5 million enforcement action against DoorDash, the largest worker protection enforcement in NYC history, with over $115 million going directly to roughly 26,000 workers. Multiple council members, including Hanif, Schulman, Restler, and Maloney, agreed that corporate accountability must accompany any regulation of worker behavior. Uber, for its part, appeared at the hearing and acknowledged competitive pressure from platforms that penalize workers for slow delivery times, while claiming it does not itself set on-time rates for two-wheeled NYC workers — a distinction the council found less than fully satisfying.

The most consequential bills debated were the proposed ban on Class 3 e-bikes (those capable of motor-assisted speeds above 20 mph), addressed in both Int 0244, which would ban their sale, and a preconsidered bill that would ban their operation after a two-year transition. DOT supported the sales ban but said it needed more time to analyze the operational ban; delivery worker advocates opposed both and proposed motor retrofitting as a cheaper alternative to a full trade-in. The trade-in program in Int 0985 was welcomed in principle but raised practical concerns — last year's battery trade-in cost roughly $5,000 per bike, and trading in the estimated 20,000 Class 3 commercial bikes could run to $100 million. Two bills attracted notable technical interest: Int 0986, which would require portable dynamometer testing to objectively verify whether a device is legal (NYPD currently cannot determine a device's maximum speed in the field, meaning legal bikes have been confiscated and illegal ones missed), and a preconsidered bill requiring shared e-bikes and scooters to install sidewalk-detection technology. Joco, a bike-share operator, testified it is already developing this technology and has demonstrated it to Chair Abreu; DOT expressed residual concern about the reliability of current detection systems but did not oppose the concept. Int 0994, requiring delivery apps to carry liability insurance, drew strong opposition from Uber, which argued it would require six times the coverage mandated for a car driver, for a 60-pound bike. Int 0950, requiring delivery services to obtain a city license, and Int 0078, requiring delivery apps to share trip-level data with the city, both received broad support from DOT and DCWP, with the latter recommending data go to DOT rather than DCWP.

Public testimony added important texture and sharpened several disagreements. Pedestrian and disability advocates described severe injuries from e-bike collisions — skull fractures, paralysis, broken bones — in nearly every case involving a rider who could not be identified because there is no license plate or registration requirement. Multiple speakers demanded passage of Priscilla's Law, which would require licensing, registration, and insurance for all e-vehicles; it was repeatedly noted that this bill was absent from the package, and no council member committed to advancing it. Borough President Reynoso and Public Advocate Williams both cautioned against disproportionate focus on e-bikes given that cars and trucks cause 95% of pedestrian deaths and serious injuries, and that New Yorkers are 42 times more likely to be injured by a car than a micromobility device. Chair Abreu acknowledged this and announced a separate Vision Zero hearing focused on car-related road violence on October 27. Immigration advocates raised serious concerns that NYPD enforcement of micromobility laws could expose undocumented delivery workers to deportation, and called for DOT to replace NYPD as the primary enforcement agency across the bill package. No resolution on this point was reached. Several EMS workers also appeared, apparently having arrived for a different hearing, to describe ambulance shortages and 45-minute response times; Chair Abreu acknowledged the issue and asked them to submit written testimony.

▸ Numbers
  • Cyclist fatalities in 2026: 20, double the figure at the same point in 2025.
  • E-bike collisions in 2026 are up 31.7% over the same period in 2025.
  • In 2025, New Yorkers were 42 times more likely to be injured by a car than by a micromobility device, and 35 times more likely to be killed.
  • Cars and trucks account for 95% of pedestrian deaths and serious injuries; cars killed 66 pedestrians in 2026 while e-bikes killed zero pedestrians.
  • Total traffic fatalities in 2026: 151, down 9 from the same point last year and down 43 from 2024.
  • E-bike and scooter rider injuries in 2026: 1,036 injured, 18 killed.
  • Of 17 e-bike fatalities in 2026, roughly 2 involved confirmed illegal devices; 6 of 11 stand-up scooter fatalities involved illegal devices.
  • NYPD issued 10,558 moving violations for e-bikes in 2026 year-to-date, versus 9,324 in all of 2025.
  • NYPD seized 126 e-bikes and 1,080 mopeds in 2026 year-to-date; total motorized device seizures reached 1,040.
  • DCWP inspected 294 unique businesses in 2026, roughly a 50% increase over all of 2025; 53 were found in violation.
  • DCWP has issued more than 460 summonses to brick-and-mortar businesses for violations of Local Laws 39 and 49 since 2023, and issued 86 cease-and-desist orders to online sellers in 2026 alone.
  • DoorDash enforcement action: $131.5 million, the largest worker protection enforcement in NYC history; over $115 million going directly to approximately 26,000 workers.
  • DCWP tipping protections have lifted delivery workers' total earnings by more than $100 million, with $184 million projected by year end.
  • Last year's battery trade-in program spent approximately $2 million for 400 bikes, roughly $5,000 per bike; trading in an estimated 20,000 Class 3 commercial bikes could cost approximately $100 million.
  • 53% of delivery workers surveyed by Workers Justice Project reported a work-related injury, a rate five times the national average; 40% reported severe psychological distress, five times the NYC adult rate.
  • 96% of surveyed delivery workers reported delivery as their only job; 52% feared deactivation for not delivering fast enough.
  • There are approximately 70,000 delivery workers in NYC completing an estimated 400,000 trips per day.
  • Cycling has grown by 60% in recent years; there are over 600,000 cycling trips per day in New York City.
  • NYPD ghost fleet operations in 2026 year-to-date: 29 joint operations, 418 arrests, 1,730 summonses.
  • Protected bike lanes reduce injury risk for cyclists by over 30%.
  • A proposed $1-per-order delivery safety surcharge would generate an estimated $146 million annually.
  • 17,000 New Yorkers have been injured in e-bike-related crashes since 2019; 133 have died.
  • Older adults represent less than 15% of the city's population but account for approximately 45% of pedestrian fatalities.
▸ Action Points
  • NYPD and DOT to provide written commitments to Chair Abreu on enforcement of the 2013 vest law requiring delivery workers to wear identifying vests.
  • DOT to analyze the operational ban on Class 3 e-bike use (preconsidered T2026-2361) and report findings to the committee.
  • DOT to review and respond to concerns about the scope and cost of the trade-in program under Int 0985, including lessons from the battery trade-in program.
  • DOT and NYPD to assess dynamometer procurement, cost, and operational logistics for field testing of micromobility devices under Int 0986 and report to the committee.
  • DOT and Joco to coordinate a formal demonstration of sidewalk-detection technology for shared micromobility devices ahead of further consideration of the preconsidered bill T2026-2590.
  • DOT to revise the draft of Int 0988 (banning deceptive advertising of illegal devices as e-bikes) to cover devices that are illegal and unregisterable, not only those capable of DMV registration.
  • DOT to confirm whether delivery trip data collected under Int 0078 should be routed to DOT rather than DCWP, and advise the committee accordingly.
  • DCWP to tailor Int 0950 (delivery service licensing) to companies using independent contractors and advise the committee on recommended amendments.
  • DCWP to include moped registration verification provisions in its enforcement framework for Int 0389 while recommending due process protections to prevent unjust worker deactivations.
  • DCWP to report to the committee on its timeline for enforcing vest and safety equipment requirements under Local Law 34, with a compliance deadline set for March 2027.
  • Chair Abreu to convene a follow-up Vision Zero hearing on October 27 focused on car-related road violence.
  • Chair Abreu to conduct a reconciliation process with advocates on disputed points in Uber's hearing testimony.
  • Council to consider amending Local Law 34 to cover temporary worker lockouts, not only permanent deactivations, in response to worker and researcher testimony.
  • DOT Commissioner Flynn to continue discussions with Lyft on age verification for Citi Bike riders following the death of a 15-year-old user, and report back to the committee.
  • Good Neighbors of Park Slope to submit a proposal to the council for adopting their rules-of-the-road campaign citywide during Bike Month in May 2027.
  • NY Immigration Coalition request to be considered by the committee: strike NYPD as enforcing agency across the bill package and amend Int 0787 to exclude immigration enforcement communications made without a warrant.
  • All non-English testimony to be translated into English for the official hearing record, as committed to by Chair Abreu.

(00:00:39)

Good morning and welcome to today's joint hearing of the New York City Council's Committees on Transportation and Infrastructure, Consumer and Worker Protection and Public Safety. I am CM Shaun Abreu, Majority Leader and Chair of the Committee on Transportation and Infrastructure. I would like to thank Speaker Menin, Chair Epstein and Chair Louis, the administration and all of you for being here today to discuss this very important topic.

To begin, I want to return to the days before we announced this hearing. A 17-year-old man, Gabriel Mercado, was on his way to work one morning on an e-bike that his uncle purchased for him. His family had been told by the seller that the machine was safe and legal. Neither were true.

The company, which encouraged riders to quote "fuel the rush," sold a bike that was faster and more powerful than permitted on our streets. Gabriel died just outside City Hall. E-bike collisions are up 31.7% this year over last year. E-bike technology has expanded in remarkable ways, expanding how New Yorkers commute, transforming our food delivery landscape and providing new recreational opportunities, but our infrastructure and policy has not kept up and there has been a deadly cost.

Before we proceed, I also want to acknowledge that there have been 10 hit-and-run deaths in New York City in less than two months. These were pedestrians and cyclists who were hit by cars that fled the scene to avoid accountability. The drivers in these cases include reckless drivers traveling at high speeds in neighborhoods known for speed racing as well as commercial drivers and trucks unsafe for busy pedestrian corridors. Even as we have prepared for this hearing, our efforts to address dangerous drivers and crashes have never slowed. On October 27, our Vision Zero hearing will include a suite of bills aimed at addressing the risk from cars. We can and will address street safety from every angle.

I will now turn it over to our Speaker, Julie Menin.

(00:02:57)

Thank you so much, Majority Leader Abreu. I really want to thank you and the other chairs, Chair Epstein and Chair Louis, and all of the staff of all three committees for their work in preparing for today's critical hearing.

Today we are examining an issue that touches the lives of millions of New Yorkers, and I think we see that from the incredible turnout from all of you today. Micro mobility devices and the need to ensure that they are used safely and responsibly on our streets — over the past two decades New York City has made tremendous progress in expanding transportation options. We have invested in bicycle infrastructure, introduced bike sharing and embraced new technologies that allow New Yorkers to move through our City more efficiently and sustainably. Motorized micro mobility devices have become commonplace on our streets, with electric bicycles, electric scooters and other devices becoming an integral part of New York City. They have created new modes of commuting, expanded access to jobs and become an essential tool for thousands of working New Yorkers. But with these opportunities comes a responsibility to ensure that our streets remain safe for everyone.

Over the years we have heard repeatedly from pedestrians who are afraid to walk on crowded sidewalks, who have been forced to look left, right and left again, literally, to feel safe crossing the street. We have seen cyclists concerned about faster motorized devices in bicycle lanes and delivery workers who face dangerous conditions while simply trying to earn a living. We have also seen the devastating consequences of serious collisions and hit-and-runs that leave families and communities in grief and in pain, and I know we are going to hear from many of them today. No New Yorkers should have to choose between getting where they need to go and feeling safe on our streets.

As Speaker, I believe the Council has a responsibility to meticulously examine the micro mobility landscape and address the major gaps in our laws that have emerged, particularly as technology and transportation habits have changed. But we also have a responsibility to hold the administration accountable for enforcing the laws that are already on the books — laws that ban the operation and the sale of high-speed uncertified devices in our City, some of which have not been enforced in decades. Several of the recent tragedies we have seen this summer involved motorized micro mobility devices that should never have been allowed to be sold, purchased or ridden in the first place.

Last month I also sent a letter to Commissioner Flynn asking that they work with Lyft to implement age verification technology for riders. Let me be clear: we are also working on legislation, which we will be releasing very soon, that will mandate that safety precaution. Other approaches, such as larger and more visible identification numbers on Citi Bike to increase accountability, should be immediately enforced. These common sense ideas can be legislated along with an educational campaign to keep minors safe.

Addressing this safety crisis is a multi-pronged effort. It requires informing consumers of the safety of the products they are advertised and sold, and ensuring that the delivery companies share responsibility for the safety of the workers who power their operations. Let me just say another word on that. Licensing — and I say this as a former Commissioner of the Department of Consumer and Worker Protection — licensing is one of the most powerful regulatory tools we have, and we as a Council intend to use it. We have bills in this package that will force the third-party apps to be licensed and put the onus on them in terms of safety, and I think that is really important.

We are also in this hearing hearing a record 23 bills. That has never been done by the City Council. This is a City Council record of 23 bills, and I really want to thank the chairs and my colleagues for all of the bills that they have put forward. Today's hearing truly reflects the Council's commitment to addressing these challenges through a comprehensive approach that brings together infrastructure enhancements, robust protections for consumers, for workers, for pedestrians and public safety principles, without needing to pit pedestrians, cyclists, delivery workers and other road users against each other.

Today we look forward to hearing from the administration, from advocates, from industry representatives, some delivery workers and from members of the public as we work to make the City's streets safer for everyone. I am going to turn it back over to Chair Abreu. I just want to mention we have multiple hearings going on. I will be stepping out right now for about 30 minutes and then will be right back to ask a lot of questions of the administration and to hear from the public. So I will turn it back over to Chair Abreu. Thank you.

One thing is clear, though: motorized micro mobility devices are here to stay. But

we need to integrate these devices more intentionally into our street design and our regulatory schemes if we want to avoid tragedies like the ones we saw far too many of this summer. The recent spate of fatal collisions is a devastating confirmation of what we already know.

High-power, high-speed devices are not safe for New York City streets. When it comes to speed, 20 is plenty. That is why the State has already laws on the books banning the sale and operation of high-speed devices, but exceptions that allow faster bikes in the City and uneven enforcement have made our streets less safe.

That is why I have introduced a package of bills to close the enforcement gap. Int 0985-2026 and Int 0986-2026 help get dangerous devices off our streets and facilitate an equitable transition to slower, safer devices for those who need them. Int 0988-2026 helps to stop dangerous devices from reaching our streets in the first place by cracking down on false advertising by retailers. Int 0989-2026, Int 1021-2026 and Int 1066-2026, as well as my preconsidered intro, demand that private companies step up and do their part in all of this by implementing stronger safety measures to protect their riders and disclosing vital safety-related data to regulators. And of course, it remains critical that the City continues to upgrade its micro mobility infrastructure to meet the needs of motorized devices, commercial riders and all New Yorkers.

Today we have a unique opportunity to bring our stakeholders together — our delivery workers, our recreational bikers, our retailers, our share system operators, our enforcement agencies and our lawmakers. I would like to thank Majority Leader Shaun Abreu and Chair Harvey Epstein for co-chairing this hearing, and I would also like to thank Speaker Julie Menin for your leadership on this very important topic: the safe usage of bikes, scooters and mopeds on the streets of New York City.

Micro mobility devices are now a regular part of New York City transportation. Scooters, bicycles and more are being used across the City every single day, now more than ever. The growth in their use makes it increasingly important that they are operated safely and responsibly and that the rules governing them are clear and effective. Micro mobility devices — bikes, scooters, small pods — provide significant benefits to our communities. They expand transportation options, reduce congestion and make daily commuting more convenient. They allow users to quickly get from point A to point B. We must do everything to encourage their use, as they provide many benefits.

At the same time, we must ensure that these devices are used safely and responsibly. When riders operate these devices recklessly — driving on sidewalks, driving the wrong way, speeding, running red lights — they create serious problems for all of us. They create foreseeable risks of injury. They also create needless worry and stress for pedestrians who deserve to feel safe on our streets. We must ensure that we do everything we can to ensure

(00:17:09)

That our laws are effective in helping us achieve our safety-related goals on this topic from the perspective of the Committee on Public Safety. I want to focus particularly on how our laws are being enforced on the street. The NYPD is responsible for enforcing many of the rules covering the operation of these devices. This includes traffic violations such as running red lights, speeding, riding the wrong way, operating on sidewalks, as well as enforcement against devices that are not legal to operate on our streets. The Committee seeks to understand how enforcement is being carried out in practice. NYPD data shows that traffic enforcement involving bicycles, bikes, scooters and mopeds has increased this year. We want to better understand how the department is enforcing speed limits and other violations involving illegal devices and how it identifies those devices when an officer encounters them on the streets.

Equally important, we also want to understand and get a full picture of the operation related to the crackdown on illegal mopeds. We know that the NYPD has been doing a lot of work on the seizure of these mopeds, something that, by the way, as we know, could be challenging. But if we seize, let us say, a thousand mopeds during the summer and a thousand new ones enter our communities, we will never, ever see any progress. What is crucial is that we think about the full picture, including how we can tackle this issue at the point of sale.

The two bills in the Committee on Public Safety, Int 0787-2026, sponsored by CM Brooks-Powers, which will require the NYPD to report on targeted traffic enforcement operations, and Int 1052-2026, sponsored by CM Vickie Paladino, which would require reporting on crashes involving micro-mobility devices, would both provide great information about where enforcement is taking place and where the crashes involving these devices are occurring.

(00:19:55)

Thank you very much. Int 0078-2026 is the retention of delivery data. I think there are around 400,000 bike deliveries every day. The Council has a different number, but that is my number. The delivery apps know how the trips are assigned and routed in real time, but the City does not. So this intro closes that gap. It gives us information about the vehicle types and the deadlines imposed by the apps, so we can understand how company practices have an impact. We do a lot of talking about the behavior, but we need to look at the companies setting the routes, the deadlines and the expectations.

Number two is Int 0095-2026, information at the point of sale. You heard about operating requirements, but that information is not necessarily where it needs to be, which is at the point of sale. Retailers would have to provide purchasers with City-prepared information. This matters as the number and variety of motorized two-wheeled vehicles on our streets continues to grow. Apparently there are 1,036 e-bike, scooter and moped riders that have been injured in crashes in 2026, and 18, as we know, have been killed.

Liability for delivery apps: Int 0994-2026, slightly controversial, but with all the 400,000 deliveries taking place, app-based delivery is no longer a small part of our transportation system. The companies that profit from using our streets need to carry responsibility when that work results in an injury or damage to property. This requires third-party food delivery companies to maintain liability insurance when a worker is making a delivery or is logged on and available to make one. The company could not obtain a City license without coverage and a lapse in coverage would void its license. I do worry about the workers. I want to make sure they are not hurt by this, but we need to work on it to make sure that there is an insurance reserve.

The Commercial E-Bike Licensing Act talks about doing that in Albany. The notion is for commercial e-bikes to have licenses — again, controversial, but it is up to Albany. Leaving the scene of a crash: Int 0044-2026. State law already says that there are consequences for motorists who leave the scene of a crash, and we are talking about more for e-scooters and asking the State to include bikes as well. This type of vehicle does not change the obligation to stop when someone has been injured. It is important to stay at the scene. Obviously we have so many bikes, scooters and mopeds and they are part of our transportation system. I want to thank everyone for being here today. I want to thank all the people who are on the podium. This is an important issue. Thank you, CM Brewer. CM Hanif, followed by CM Hudson.

(00:23:00)

Thank you. Good morning, everyone. Thank you to Chairs Epstein and Feliz for holding today's important hearing and for including my bill, Int 0214-2026, on the agenda. Int 0214-2026 represents a true partnership between our community in District 39 and local government. This introduction is credited to the tireless organizing of the passionate volunteers of Good Neighbors of Park Slope. Thank you to Kathy and Lenore for being here today and to the many members who have already submitted testimony in support of this bill. Good Neighbors first approached my office months ago, wanting to turn their concerns around pedestrian and bike safety into action. Together we designed and ran a multilingual campaign across LinkNYC kiosks and introduced Int 0214-2026 as part of that civic engagement collaboration.

Int 0214-2026 largely codifies the rules of the road requirements that Citi Bike already shares with its riders, requiring micro-mobility operators to have their riders review the rules of the road before unlocking or activating a shared bike or scooter. New users would receive this information when they first use the system and existing users would review it at least once each year. The bill would also require rules of the road signage at micro-mobility share stations. Those rules include the basics we should all expect riders to know: yielding to pedestrians, obeying stop signs and traffic signals, riding in the direction of traffic, understanding where bikes and scooters can be ridden, using a bell, restrictions on earphones and operating at a safe speed.

As we go into this hearing featuring nearly two dozen bills and resolutions, I want to be crystal clear: any conversation about micro-mobility safety must meaningfully engage the more than 70,000 delivery workers who rely on these devices to make a living. Our City has created a crisis on our streets by meeting a ballooning on-demand delivery economy with infrastructure that has failed to keep pace with this new reality. The very real safety concerns we hear from constituents are also the result of political choices and years of inaction. That is why the onus of e-bike reforms cannot fall solely at the feet of a precarious workforce whose working conditions, schedules and routes are largely determined...

(00:25:37)

...by powerful tech companies. 53% of delivery workers surveyed by CUNY and Workers Justice Project reported a workplace-related injury. When your risk of injury on the job is about the same as a coin flip, that is not simply a cost of doing business. Tech oligarchs have built a business model around algorithms that present delivery workers with an impossible choice: speed your way there or risk deactivation. The current state of play is untenable. Every time we order in, an algorithm pressures this vulnerable workforce to literally hurl themselves into traffic in the name of shareholder value, and that has to stop.

We want to have our cake delivered by Grubhub and eat it too. Micro-mobility devices are increasingly becoming a staple on our streets. According to Transportation Alternatives, one in ten New Yorkers reports using one and 40% of people in bike lanes are riding them. We must thoughtfully regulate these devices and the companies that profit from their use. That conversation cannot begin with the loudest voices calling for incarceration, fines and confiscations. We must lead with robust City planning, fair scheduling, education, trade-ins, retrofits, compassion and dignity. I look forward to hearing from the agency staff before us, my constituents of Good Neighbors of Park Slope and members of the public as we have this important discussion about micro-mobility safety. Thank you.

(00:27:18)

Thank you so much, Chairs, for holding this important hearing today and for the opportunity to speak on my bills. Good morning, everyone. Int 0244-2026 would prohibit the sale and rental of Class 3 e-bikes. Any seller or lessor who violates the law, including companies like Whizz or Sumo, which many delivery workers use, would see a fine of up to $2,000. No penalties would be levied against any buyer or renter. Bikes that can be sold or rented can only go up to 20 miles per hour and this keeps many bikes that are popular with delivery workers, like the Fly 11, legal to sell and available for purchase.

The second bill, Preconsidered Int T2026-2361, would ban use of Class 3 e-bikes after a two-year implementation period, with civil penalties beginning in spring 2029. With a lengthy implementation period, workers would have time to utilize their existing bikes until the end of their useful life, at which point they could either buy a new bike compliant with Int 0244-2026, rent an e-bike, or add a speed limiter or lower-powered motor to effectively convert non-compliant bikes to a Class 2 e-bike. Importantly, there are no criminal penalties.

Together these bills meet the objective of removing the fastest bikes from our streets while providing ample opportunity for those who currently use Class 3 bikes for work to make necessary adjustments. As a governing body we have a responsibility to make our streets safer, but the bills we are hearing today are only part of the solution. Perhaps more importantly, we have a responsibility to protect the workers who use those streets every day. I worry that some of the rhetoric around this package risks putting the blame on delivery workers when they are often the ones facing the greatest risks and calling the loudest for greater safety and accountability. Delivery apps impose aggressive delivery times and threaten deactivation, pressuring workers to speed. We need to hold these companies accountable for the incentives they create, not just the workers navigating them. We cannot leave out the need for greater infrastructure investments like delivery hubs, charging stations and protected bike lanes that keep everyone safe, namely delivery workers and pedestrians.

When we take a both-and legislative approach rather than one that limits us to either-or, we can solve for the devices that are too fast for our streets and at the same time support the people who depend on them to make a living. These bills address one part of the problem. They are not the whole solution and our goal should be to build a city where pedestrians are safe, riders are safe and delivery workers are not forced to choose between their safety and their livelihood. Thank you. CM Schulman, followed by CM Restler. Thank you.

(00:39:04)

Good morning. I am Mike Flynn, Commissioner of the New York City Department of Transportation. Good morning, Speaker Menin, Chair Feliz and members of the Committees on Transportation and Infrastructure, Consumer and Worker Protection and Public Safety. With me today is Madeline Labadie, my Chief of Staff. Thank you for the opportunity to testify on behalf of Mayor Mamdani about micro-mobility in New York City and the administration's work to enhance safety for micro-mobility riders, pedestrians and all road users.

The use of electric micro-mobility devices such as e-bikes and scooters has dramatically increased in recent years. One of my top priorities is creating safe and reliable infrastructure that supports growing ridership and allows even more New Yorkers to feel comfortable getting around on bikes. With record growth in bike ridership, including more than 600,000 cycling trips taken in the City each day and an increasing share of riders choosing to ride bikes or e-scooters, we know we need to think holistically to meet this growth while improving safety for everyone on our streets. When used safely and legally, bikes offer affordable, sustainable and convenient options for a range of New Yorkers, from families to delivery workers, tens of thousands of whom rely on micro-mobility for their livelihoods. The more we can shift New Yorkers to more sustainable, more space-efficient options, the safer and healthier our communities will be and the less congestion we all suffer. These devices have made travel easier, improved accessibility and provided new economic opportunities. But with this shift in the way people get around our City, we need infrastructure, laws and public education to keep up.

We want New York City to be the global model for what safe, legal micro-mobility can be and to provide New Yorkers with more options to get around without needing to own a car. One challenge is that the evolving market of micro-mobility devices often leaves riders and consumers confused. It is difficult for consumers to know the difference between legal e-bikes, illegal e-bikes, mopeds, motorcycles, et cetera. The law includes three classes of bikes right now. Two of them have max speeds of 20 miles per hour and the third has a max speed of 25 miles per hour. Any e-bike that goes above these speeds is illegal. Yet many consumers are misled by deceptive marketing practices by retailers who advertise and sell illegal products. This is why we recently joined Mayor Mamdani in the issuance of cease and desist letters to 42 retailers found to be selling illegal e-bikes to New Yorkers online.

While nearly 90% of traffic deaths involve cars and trucks, micro-mobility-related fatalities and injuries are a serious concern. Two of the biggest factors in the...

(00:41:51)

...severity of a traffic crash are the speed and weight of a vehicle, which is why the proliferation of faster, heavier illegal e-bikes and stand-up scooters is such an alarming trend on our streets. More than half of all riders who die in a traffic crash are riding illegal devices capable of going at high speed. This is dangerous and unacceptable. Earlier this year, two lives were lost when an illegal high-speed scooter capable of going more than 50 miles per hour collided with a cyclist on the Queensboro Bridge. This should have never happened and the companies that illegally sell these products must be held accountable.

While we focus on reining in the supply of these devices, we are also continuing our educational efforts. We distribute the chart included in my printed testimony to the public to explain the differences between various devices, including which ones are legal and which ones are illegal. Emerging technology is continuing to change the landscape of how New Yorkers get around the City and we are focused on creatively and strategically meeting the moment during this time of rapid change on our streets. We are embracing the new opportunities presented by micro-mobility while addressing challenges too — pedestrian, rider and fire safety — in a safe and equitable way.

First and foremost, we are focused on roadway safety and striving towards our Vision Zero goals. Recent high-profile fatal crashes and serious injuries have reinforced the urgency of this work. We are ramping up the installation of innovative street redesigns that reimagine our streets to prioritize pedestrians, mass transit and cycling — interventions that are making our streets safer and the planet greener. We are improving micro-mobility infrastructure by expanding our network of protected bike lanes, including wider protected bike lanes and the establishment of new pedestrianized space. We are also exploring other treatments to enhance street safety through design, including signal timing modifications, expanded outdoor bike parking, cargo bike corrals, loading zones for e-micro-mobility and bike boulevards to accommodate these modes. We know infrastructure works and can see the safety impact wherever we put in proven design interventions. These designs also make streets safer for all users. Protected and standard bike lanes in New York City, for example, have been shown to reduce injury risk for cyclists by over 30%.

Along with design interventions, we are also working to address fire risks from micro-mobility batteries. We are doing this by implementing fire-safe battery swapping cabinets on public sidewalks and plazas. These cabinets and the batteries associated with them will be certified to the highest nationally recognized fire safety standard and will include proactive monitoring of battery health along with fire containment features. Over 100 delivery workers participated in the pilot and one shared that the new infrastructure and certified batteries gave them peace of mind as they kept their family and neighbors safe.

We have also been taking meaningful steps towards our mission to provide the safe, equitable and sustainable movement of goods and people citywide through our freight mobility and sustainable delivery work. We continue to encourage companies to use commercial cargo bicycles to make local and last-mile deliveries across the City and in recent years authorized the use of e-cargo bikes along with key safety standards. Cargo bikes have a direct benefit on safety and sustainability as each bike replaces a truck or van that would otherwise deliver those goods to the final customer.

In addition to freight mobility, we are also taking action to hold third-party delivery companies accountable for their impact on street safety and provide training and resources for delivery workers. Roadway safety is important for all New Yorkers and for delivery workers. The roadway is their workplace. Our efforts, with support from our strong partnerships with delivery worker stakeholders, improve street safety and simultaneously improve the safety and dignity of delivery work. For too long, third-party delivery companies had, through algorithmic management and the threat of deactivation, created working conditions that lead to dangerous cycling behavior. As such, we are committed to improving street safety in ways that take into account the pressures faced by delivery workers.

In this spirit, we continue to build our Sustainable Delivery Unit to address the street safety challenges associated with the rapid growth of on-demand delivery services. Our Sustainable Delivery Unit works to strengthen oversight of delivery apps, enhance safety for delivery workers and the public through education and outreach and analyze the impacts of the delivery industry on our streets. Through DOT's training portal developed by this team, thousands of delivery workers have already taken the bicycle safety course focused on the rules of the road and safe operation of bikes. We continue to make strides towards our goal of ensuring all delivery workers have taken our safety training. Finally, we are also developing policy to ensure that all delivery service companies transition their workforce towards legal and compliant devices. As dangerous devices proliferate, these companies must do their part to ensure their deliveries are completed on safe devices.

Turning to the legislation before the Council today. First, Int 0078-2026, sponsored by CM Brewer. This Bill would require third-party delivery services licensed by the City to share granular travel data for all delivery trips made by their contracted delivery workers. We support this Bill as requiring trip-level data from delivery platforms will bolster the administration's ability to examine the transportation and street safety impacts of the delivery worker environment. With DOT's mission to provide for the safe, equitable and sustainable movement of people and goods, we also recommend amending the Bill to grant access to this travel data to assess the industry's impact on street safety for all street users, not just delivery workers. We also recommend that the Bill be amended to include crash data, which would further support our efforts to understand the industry's impact on street safety.

Next, Int 0095-2026, sponsored by CM Brewer. This Bill would require DOT to develop materials containing information related to the registration, inspection, operation and traffic safety requirements for mopeds and would require moped retailers to distribute such materials at the point of sale. We are proud to have supported passage of the state law to require ownership information at the point of sale and have found it to be effective thus far. DOT already promotes a robust suite of educational materials, safety information and events to help people ride mopeds more safely. We support this Bill and look forward to working with our sister agency and the Council to strengthen ongoing outreach efforts.

Next, Int 0110-2026, sponsored by CM Brooks-Powers. This Bill would expand commercial delivery safety and training requirements to cover all two-wheel devices. Cyclists using bikes, e-bikes and cargo bikes are already required to receive safety gear and occupational safety training and we support the need to expand this coverage for workers who use mopeds, motorcycles and scooters, which would further strengthen safety requirements covering more delivery devices used by workers. We have suggestions for the safety requirements outlined in the proposed legislation and we look forward to working with the Council to strengthen safety standards.

Next, Int 0214-2026, sponsored by CM Hanif. This Bill would require bikeshare operators to display rules of the road on their mobile applications and on physical stations. The...

(00:49:28)

Operators would be required to ensure that users review the rules at least once per year before activating or unlocking a device. We believe that our bike and micro mobility share partners play an important role in educating riders and support the intent of the Bill. Road rules are already posted on every Citi Bike kiosk and inside the basket of every Citi Bike. Even so, DOT believes that app platforms are best suited for educating riders. All of our partners include safety information and road rules within their apps currently, but we look forward to discussing with the Council how those can be strengthened.

Next, Int 0244, sponsored by CM Hudson. This Bill would prohibit the sale and rental of Class 3 bicycles with electric assist. Class 3 bikes can create additional safety challenges compared to lighter and slower e-bikes. In the rest of the state, all vehicles capable of travelling over 20 miles per hour are required to have a license and registration to operate. We support prohibiting the sale and rental of these devices to help reduce their prevalence on the street. We will be pursuing state legislation to create e-bike regulations consistent with national and international safety standards. To accomplish this, we will work with advocates in New York State to advance a regulatory framework that transitions New Yorkers and delivery workers away from devices capable of traveling more than 20 miles per hour. We will also continue to work with delivery workers through outreach and education efforts to incentivize riders to transition to devices that ride below 20 miles per hour. We support this Bill and look forward to ongoing efforts to strengthen regulations in the e-bike marketplace.

Next, Int 0095, sponsored by CM Restler. This Bill would require third party food delivery services to verify the registration of mopeds used by food delivery workers. Mopeds are already required to be registered at the point of sale and we support the intent of this Bill, as we want to ensure third party delivery apps are held accountable to also verify registration while also working with our sister agency to mitigate potential worker protection concerns.

Next, Int 0389, sponsored by CM Brooks-Powers. This Bill requires reporting on targeted traffic enforcement operations by the police department. We maintain a strong partnership with the NYPD on data that supports street safety measures. We defer to the NYPD on the details of the Bill.

Next, Int 0950, sponsored by CM... This Bill would require delivery services that facilitate or arrange for the delivery of goods in the City to obtain a license. We support the intent of a more level playing field among delivery service companies, which would include all major third party delivery services that rely on these devices to obtain a license, in an effort to comprehensively address micro mobility safety and the delivery industry. We defer to our sister agency for details.

Next, Int 0905, sponsored by Chair Abreu. This Bill would establish a trade-in program for Class 3 bicycles with electric assist and vehicles that resemble but do not legally qualify as Class 1 or Class 2 e-bikes. While we support the Bill's intent to encourage New Yorkers to replace unsafe and illegal vehicles with compliant micro mobility devices, we have concerns with the Bill as written. Standing up a program like this is very resource intensive and a narrower approach that targets the most dangerous illegal devices could prove a greater impact on street safety. As we finalize a full evaluation of last year's e-bike trade-in program, we look forward to sharing lessons learned to inform a program with Council support to best enhance street safety for all road users.

Next, Int 0987, sponsored by Chair Abreu. This Bill would require the use of micro mobility dynamometers to test micro mobility devices operating in the City, followed by a report of data gathered through the testing. We support the Bill's intent to ensure all riders use safe, compliant devices. We are interested in testing any technology that can help increase the use of safe devices on our streets. At the same time, we have heard concerns about equity and implementation and we would defer on those concerns. We look forward to working with the Council and the NYPD to develop best practices for implementation.

Next, Int 0988, sponsored by Chair Abreu. This Bill would prohibit the advertisement of vehicles required to be registered with the Department of Motor Vehicles as powered bicycles or powered mobility devices. We support this Bill to prohibit misleading advertisements, but the current draft of the Bill only targets the advertisement of legal devices, given that they must be registered. We think it is also important to expand the Bill to also prohibit advertising illegal devices as e-bikes or other legal mobility devices, such as in the case of the most recent crash in front of One Centre Street. These illegal devices cannot be registered and therefore would not be captured by the Bill as drafted.

Next, Int 0989, sponsored by Chair Abreu. This Bill would require the City's bikeshare operator to report safety incidents involving their devices, followed by a report published to the web. Citi Bike is now an integral part of New York City's transportation system. Given its growing role in our transportation system, understanding how it contributes to the safety landscape is critical to enhancing safety on our streets. We support the intent of this Bill, but understand there are operational challenges to collecting some of this data for the system operator, such as incident data being self-reported by riders. Citi Bike's existing program agreement already includes robust requirements to report known safety incidents.

Next, Int 0994, sponsored by CM Brewer. This Bill requires third party food delivery services to maintain liability insurance. We support efforts to ensure that delivery services carry appropriate insurance to protect New Yorkers when there are incidents on the job. Workers and pedestrians should not be left to pay for injuries and damages caused by collisions on the job. Delivery apps need to acknowledge their role and bear the responsibility for the externalities of their business.

Everything we do prioritizes pedestrian safety and we completely understand the need.

This Bill requires the installation of traffic safety tracking technology on devices used by delivery workers with a progressive penalty structure. We defer to our sister agency on the details and look forward to future conversations on how best to promote safe riding behavior.

Lastly, Preconsidered T2026-2590, sponsored by Chair Abreu. This Bill requires the installation of sidewalk riding detection technology on the bikes and scooters that are part of self-service share systems, with violators facing a progressive penalty structure. E-bike and e-scooter riders must abide by the same traffic laws, rules and regulations applicable to drivers. Riders are permitted on arterial and local streets throughout the City and must use designated bike lanes when available. Riding is prohibited on certain bridges, expressways and highways unless a designated bicycle path exists. In general, riders are not permitted to operate on sidewalks, but we do have locations across the City that allow for sidewalk riding as indicated by official signage. We support the Bill's intent so that riders abide by these rules, but based on our research, existing technology cannot effectively distinguish between sidewalk and road surface and can cause safety issues for riders in the road if the technology mistakes them for sidewalk riders, slowing the device while in traffic. We share the Council's concern with unsafe riding behavior and look forward to working with the sponsor to discuss existing and reliable safety tools to address this concern.

In conclusion, every death or serious injury on our streets is an avoidable tragedy affecting not only the victim, but their families, friends and colleagues. Since I began serving as Commissioner, I have met many New Yorkers who lost loved ones to traffic violence or were themselves injured in a crash. Hearing their stories and understanding the long-term pain and trauma that never really go away makes these issues not just academic, but so very tangible. It underscores the importance of our work together here today towards Vision Zero. Likewise, every member of our team that works on Vision Zero and street safety projects carries with them a profound sense of responsibility to not only honor the memory of those who have been lost with aggressive, data-driven action, but to prevent the next tragedy from happening.

(01:02:32)

Vision Zero works. It is saving lives. Compared to the same point in the year before Vision Zero was implemented, traffic deaths are down nearly 30%. But this is about more than numbers and percentages. These are real people, real families. That 30% reduction in traffic deaths means more than 50 individuals are alive who would not have been since Vision Zero began. That is 50 individuals, 50 families and many more times more neighbors, coworkers and classmates of those individuals, all of whom will be spared from tragedy. We are encouraged by the progress we are making at DOT, but acknowledge that there is much more to be done. I would like to thank the Council for the opportunity to testify before you today and I look forward to partnering together on meaningful legislation to help shape evolving micro mobility trends in a safe and impactful way. We would now be happy to pass to the next testimony. Thank you.

(01:03:28)

Thank you, Commissioner Flynn. Good morning, Chairs Epstein and Abreu, and members of the Committee on Consumer and Worker Protection, the Committee on Transportation and Infrastructure and the Committee on Public Safety. I am Sam Levine, Commissioner of the Department of Consumer and Worker Protection. I am joined this morning by our Chief of Staff and Deputy Commissioner for External Affairs, Carlos Ortiz. Thank you for the opportunity to testify today before the committees on micro mobility in New York City and related legislation.

The New York City Department of Consumer and Worker Protection is the nation's leading municipal enforcement agency charged with delivering economic justice. In the last nine months, the Mamdani Administration has demonstrated New York City's commitment to refuse to allow multibillion-dollar companies to rake in record profits on the backs of New Yorkers. We have been at the forefront of this all-hands-on-deck effort, protecting New Yorkers from corporate tricks and traps that make their lives harder. I am proud to report that through our programs, protections and policies, we have secured more than $1 billion on behalf of New Yorkers thus far in 2026.

During this administration, I have made engaging New Yorkers a priority across all of the services and programs that we offer as an agency. We hear directly from New Yorkers on the issues that matter most to them. This includes direct outreach to consumers, to small businesses, to workers and partnerships with our sister agencies such as my colleagues at the Department of Transportation. In 2026, we have conducted nearly 800 outreach events and have engaged with nearly 200,000 New Yorkers through direct public awareness efforts and financial empowerment programming. We remain committed and steadfast in our promise to the communities of our City that

(01:05:12)

We are here as a partner and ally and stand ready to champion their rights. Now turning to the topic of today's hearing. At the start of this year we celebrated the passage of laws to advance protections for delivery workers, including tipping protections and an expansion of the minimum pay rate. Our workers' tipping report, which showed two major engineered design tricks to make it harder for consumers to tip and drove down worker pay by more than half a billion dollars, exemplified the need for these laws. More recent reports show that these new tipping protections have lifted workers' total earnings on delivery by more than a hundred million dollars and are on course to hit $184 million by the end of the year. Earlier this year we secured more than $5 million from three major delivery apps and reinstatement for up to 10,000 wrongfully terminated workers.

This work is only accelerating. Just last week we announced a historic $131.5 million enforcement action against Doordash, the largest worker protection enforcement in the history of New York City, for systemic violations of New York City's delivery worker laws. More than $115 million will go directly to over 26,000 delivery workers. By arming workers with tools to report trip data directly to us, we are both investing in worker power and ensuring that these massive violations will not recur in our five boroughs.

Now, a common tactic by companies, often to distract from their track record of mistreating workers, is to pit workers' rights against street safety. Nothing could be further from the truth. I speak with delivery workers all the time and they consistently describe the pressure to complete more orders in less time or risk deactivation. As CM Hudson said earlier, no workers should have to choose between their safety and their livelihood. That is why the Mamdani Administration was proud to support and will be vigorously enforcing Local Law 34 of 2026, which puts an end to arbitrary deactivations in New York City.

Today the Council is considering numerous bills aimed at strengthening street safety and we look forward to a thoughtful discussion of each. But our administration — and I speak for all the agencies here — believes strongly that the basic deactivation protections provided by Local Law 34 are a critical part of any street safety effort. No amount of training, equipment requirements or monitoring can make our streets meaningfully safer if workers are still operating under constant pressure to race against the clock or risk losing their jobs. Street safety requires not only addressing how delivery workers ride, but the incentives and pressures that shape how they are required to work.

Turning now to our enforcement efforts: we enforce various laws that protect consumers from the sale of unsafe and illegal micromobility devices. We aim to keep our City free from these devices as the lead enforcement agency for Local Laws 39, 49 and 50, and we also actively enforce against the sale of oversized and overpowered motorized scooters and illegal mopeds. Following the passage of these laws we sprang into action, conducting enforcement sweeps of retailers across the five boroughs and working swiftly to bring them into compliance. We continue to conduct regular proactive enforcement in this space. Just this past summer, our inspectors and attorneys conducted a comprehensive sweep of micromobility retailers across the City, working to ensure continued compliance with our laws. Since we began enforcing these laws in 2023, we have conducted nearly 1,000 inspections and issued more than 460 summonses to brick-and-mortar businesses for violations of Local Law 39, Local Law 49 and the motorized scooter law. In 2026 we issued over 85 cease and desist orders and 41 summonses to online sellers for violations of Local Law 39. We have also targeted eight locations for sealing, sealed three of them and brought five into compliance prior to having to seal them.

Turning to today's legislation and starting with Int 0078-2026: this Bill requires licensed delivery services to submit data for each trip made in the City. Our administration supports this legislation to require third-party food delivery services to submit data regularly. However, we and our sister agencies are aligned that DOT, which is best positioned to oversee the City's street safety laws and indeed already does, should receive this data. The administration looks forward to working with the Council on this legislation.

Turning to Int 0244-2026, which would prohibit the sale and rental of Class B e-bikes: we support this Bill, which would ban the sale and rental of certain throttle e-bikes with higher speed capacity. However, we have operational concerns related to online and field enforcement challenges, as not all products accurately post or specify speed information. We look forward to hearing from stakeholders today and working with the Council and our sister agencies on this Bill.

Turning to Int 0095-2026: this would prohibit third-party food delivery services from permitting any food delivery worker hired, retained or engaged by the third-party food delivery service to utilize a moped unless the delivery service has verified registration of the moped. Our administration supports the intent of this legislation to require third-party food delivery services to verify moped registration. However, we have concerns that the Bill as written could result in delivery workers being unfairly deactivated by delivery apps. Unless there are reasonable guardrails in place, workers should be given notice and an opportunity to address any issues with their vehicle registration and the chance to get back to work once this is addressed. We recommend adding language requiring warnings and other progressive discipline measures to ensure workers are not permanently deactivated arbitrarily, and providing for an extended effective date. We look forward to working with the Council and our sister agencies to establish a balanced approach that addresses both street safety priorities and workers' rights and their need to earn a living.

Turning to Int 0214-2026: this Bill would require delivery services that facilitate or arrange for the delivery of goods in the City to obtain a business license, which would set forth conditions on such a license and enforcement options. We support the intent of this Bill to expand the coverage of its third-party food delivery service license. However, we recommend that we tailor the Bill to require licensing for companies that engage contracted delivery workers as independent contractors, so that we focus on the appropriate companies and do not unintentionally burden small businesses. We have started 2026 with aggressive enforcement against powerful corporations that are exploiting workers and we stand ready to continue that fight.

Turning to Int 0988-2026, which would prohibit the advertisement for sale, lease, rent or distribution of any vehicle that can be registered with New York State as a street-legal bike or scooter: the Bill requires us to develop and distribute information on unauthorized e-bikes and scooters to retailers to post and offer to consumers. We support this Bill, which prohibits the advertisement for sale, lease or rental. However, there are policy and fiscal concerns about the breadth of outreach contemplated by this Bill and the significant investment that would be required to conduct it. We look forward to working to ensure that any new campaign is coordinated with the appropriate agencies who have similar campaigns and have additional subject matter expertise. We look forward to working with the Council on refinements to this Bill.

Turning to Int 0989-2026: this Bill would require certain third-party food delivery services to maintain insurance to cover liability

(01:13:28)

that arises from accidents involving a delivery worker on an e-bike, scooter or illegal moped while the delivery worker is making a delivery or is available to make a delivery for the third-party food delivery service. We support the intent of this Bill to require apps to maintain liability coverage for accidents involving a delivery worker on micromobility devices. We look forward to hearing more from stakeholders on this Bill today.

Turning to Int 1021-2026, which would require third-party food delivery services to submit an annual safety report: the administration supports this Bill but believes these reports and plans should be provided to DOT, which is best positioned to leverage that information for street safety enforcement and overall strategic planning efforts.

Turning now to Preconsidered Bill T2026-2361: this would prohibit the operation of Class B e-bikes, require that violators receive a warning for the first violation and escalating civil penalties for subsequent violations. We share the Council's goal to rid our streets of unsafe micromobility devices. However, we have significant concerns about unintended consequences that would most heavily impact delivery workers, many of whom are immigrants with limited English proficiency and other vulnerabilities. Delivery workers could face losing their livelihoods as well as increased interactions with law enforcement at a time when many are especially fearful of such interactions, and this could also lead to confiscation of the device they use every day to earn a living. We look forward to working with the Council to improve street safety while balancing the needs of some of our City's most essential yet vulnerable workers.

Turning now to Preconsidered Bill T2026-2587: this would require third-party delivery services to use tracking technology to detect when certain delivery workers engage in unsafe traffic behaviors while making a delivery or while available to make a delivery. Generally, we have concerns about the idea of requiring constant electronic surveillance of workers who are primarily immigrants. We also have concerns that as drafted, this Bill could lead to delivery workers being unfairly deactivated by delivery apps. We look forward to working with the Council to further street safety and worker safety goals while limiting unintended consequences that flow from this legislation.

In conclusion, I want to thank the Council sincerely for the opportunity to testify before these committees on today's legislation and for the Council's attention to these vital issues. Our administration looks forward to hearing from stakeholders today on the impact of these bills and we look forward to collaborating closely with the Council during the legislative process. I welcome any questions you have and now turn things over to our Police Department. Thank

you. We look forward to answering any questions you might have.

Just this past Monday, a woman was struck and killed by a truck driver who then left the scene. Can you describe NYPD's seizure process from beginning to end? For example, how is the determination to confiscate a device made — I think you were getting into that with Chief Obey earlier in your testimony — where is the device taken, and when and how is the device destroyed, and does the rider have any opportunity to appeal or retrieve their device? Walk me through the beginning: the decision-making to confiscate, where the device is taken, and when and how the device is destroyed.

(01:48:30)

Sure. Building off of what Chief Obey was saying a moment ago: the initial seizure happens for a variety of different reasons, and that will affect the path it takes. So it could be because it is being used in connection with a felony or misdemeanor. We do see that sometimes. It could be seized pursuant to the Administrative Code — that is 19-196.2(d) — as a motorized scooter when it is being used in a dangerous manner. It could also be an unregistered motor vehicle under the VTL. So when that happens, the device is going to be taken initially to the precinct of occurrence. There it is going to be vouchered — documenting, you know, where the device was taken from. And assuming we know who it was taken from, it will be stored initially at the command. At some point it will be transported to a property clerk facility, which typically would be the pound. Now in terms of the process after that...

(01:49:47)

The question would be whether we get a request to return it or we do not get a request for return — that will take different paths. So let us assume no request has ever been made to get the device back. It will be held: if it was taken as arrest evidence in connection with a felony or misdemeanor, we will wait until determination of the underlying criminal matter plus 151 days. Otherwise, if it was seized pursuant to that Admin Code provision I mentioned or pursuant to the VTL, 90 days. If after that time period no one has claimed it, it is going to be destroyed.

If someone comes to request the return of a device: if it was seized as arrest evidence, obviously it will wait until the underlying matter is resolved. The person has to provide proof of adjudication — let us say it was a criminal summons. If it is under the Admin Code or the VTL, again, assuming there was an OATH summons or a summons, that summons has to be adjudicated. The person comes to the command with evidence of that. And the last point I want to make is that depending on the situation — if you have something that is an unauthorized motor vehicle — they cannot just ride it away, because you cannot ride it on the streets of New York. So in that situation, the person either has to show up with the relevant license and registration, or they come with a flatbed truck and can put it on the truck. But we cannot...

(01:51:31)

let someone ride away on a device that is unlawful to ride on the streets of New York City.

At the end of the day, if workers feel that they can be deactivated arbitrarily, without cause, for say not hitting those delivery times, we are never going to have safety on the streets because we are forcing people to choose between their livelihoods and their safety. We are also strong supporters, as you know, of the Council's minimum pay rate, which we have been implementing and enforcing aggressively. That is part of having a more professional delivery workforce. The apps wanted to have a model where they bring in tens of thousands of people to occasionally do deliveries, maybe not trained on what is safe, maybe not trained on the legal devices. Because a moped, whether it is Class B or Class C, is like a motor vehicle or motorcycle. It is actually a class of motorcycle, which is a class of motor vehicle. It has to be licensed, has to be registered, has to have insurance. So if you have a moped with no license, or a license that is not legitimate, or if it is not registered, that is subject to enforcement action. You are going to have a summons and be subject to a seizure.

As you know, we tightened up our vehicle pursuit policy, and for good reasons. A lot of that enforcement is going to involve trying to engage with someone when they are stopped, or when the moped is parked on the street or attached to something, and then the officers are in a position to do the assessment as best they possibly can: is this a lawful moped?

With respect to motorized scooters, we issued summonses for violations of the motorized scooter law in 2026. So we are encouraged by the trends, but we are going to continue aggressive compliance sweeps to ensure that these noncompliance figures continue to go down. Do we currently have sufficient authority to prevent devices from being sold online?

(02:54:58)

I think it is harder online, and that is why we have been working with the Department of Transportation. You saw the announcement by the Mayor and I believe by Commissioner Flynn a couple of months ago about a pretty significant enforcement sweep. We certainly issue summonses to online businesses. We do that all the time. If they are outside of the City that can be harder to serve, but we are working closely on that.

(02:55:28)

Well, I think on the cease and desist for online sales, so far we have been very encouraged. I do not have the exact number, but I think over a dozen online retailers have already complied and we are in active discussions with most of the remainder. We have also learned that some online retailers are more scrupulous than others, and if we see things like not including the specs or other ways to make it harder... As Commissioner Levin said, it is definitely more tricky on the online side of it. And then of course there is the secondary sales market too. So it just underscores the need for a real nuanced approach, and I think a lot of the Bills being considered today can help us keep evolving.

(02:56:19)

How many summonses have been given due to the sale of these devices online? I believe we have issued 86 cease and desist letters, and we have issued — is that for 2026? — and then we have issued 41 summonses to online sellers that do not comply with the cease and desist letters. I think that is cumulative.

Okay. A few final questions on the trade-in programs that we have been working on these past few years. A few years ago we also passed the trade-in legislation requiring a study to create a program where riders are able to trade in their uncertified bikes or batteries for one that is certified and safe. Can you provide any updates on that, including how many certified batteries you have been able to provide?

(02:57:15)

Yes, sure. Well, I think you know that that local law — I believe it was Local Law 95, I may be mistaken — it provided the companies with a lot of flexibility on how they could comply with the law. There were different models they could use, whether it was replacing bikes or offering some discounts on rental subscriptions, for example, so it gave them a lot of flexibility in how they could comply. I think towards the end of last year we had worked with our colleagues at City Hall to issue letters to all the companies letting them know that they were on notice and had to be in compliance. We followed up earlier this year to ensure that they would map out for us their plans for compliance as well. Thus far we have received no complaints from a worker about not receiving some type of vehicle, either through the rental subscription model or not. I think most of the apps, if not all, are using the rental subscription model as how to comply with this. That said, I think there are ways we can strengthen this access to safe devices and I know our partners are working on that actively as well.

(02:58:23)

OK, and going back to the issue related to enforcement at the point of sale, can you talk to us about the procedures and operations for us? These are the rules related to the point of sale. I know this could be challenging, so just wondering what the procedures are.

(02:58:38)

It is not challenging at all. It is something that we have done since 2021, and again in phase one we do this in phases. At that time the focus was on mopeds. We transitioned in January 2026 to high-powered and out-of-class scooters. So again, we do this work — first we tapped into our local precincts, 78 of them citywide, to survey their command and identify retailers that are selling these devices. They came back to us with 71 retailers identified citywide selling these illegal devices. We reach out to them, they do the educational outreach part of it. We provided a flyer, and that flyer is instrumental to us in the sense that we provided it to our officers to help make that distinction between whether a device is legal or non-street-legal. It is helpful, just some of that language around it, and we also provided that to the retailers. So there has been notice given to these retailers. After we do that with the flyers, then inspections start, and then enforcement. Those are the steps that we have taken. As I mentioned, 63 retail stores were inspected as of September 21. From those 63 retail stores, 90 bikes were seized from various locations, and again this is citywide. Nine summonses were issued for motorized scooter noncompliance. 57 criminal summonses were issued for other retail violations, for the most part at repeat locations. We have to expand this and we will do so, but again we work hand in hand on this.

(03:40:40)

Not yet. Thank you, Chair, and to all of the chairs and members of the committees for hearing my legislation as part of today's package. Our streets are changing fast. E-biking serves as an essential mode of transportation for New Yorkers every day and a key source of income for thousands of delivery workers, but illegally operated, uncertified vehicles have raised real and urgent public safety concerns. The two bills I am sponsoring today, Int 0110-2026 and Int 0787-2026, help hold accountable the businesses that profit from the delivery system and increase reporting and transparency on how the City conducts traffic enforcement on these vehicles.

Int 0110-2026 would expand the City's commercial bicycle laws to cover all electric bicycles, mopeds and motorcycles. This places responsibility for the safety and oversight of these vehicles where it belongs: on the businesses and apps that profit from delivery. Businesses that use such delivery services for commercial purposes would be required to provide safety equipment, identification and safety information to their delivery workers, as well as maintain records of their delivery workers. Int 0787-2026 would require the NYPD to report quarterly on its targeted traffic enforcement: where these operations occur, how the decision to conduct them is made, the number of summonses issued, and specifically enforcement directed at e-bike riders, with a year-over-year comparison.

As part of this full package, the City will work to enforce safer streets, and this Bill allows the public and this Council to know how it is happening and who is being targeted. In previous hearings of the Committee on Transportation and Infrastructure, we were unable to get clear data on where e-bike and scooter violations were most prevalent. So this Bill aims to bring that data forward. Together, these bills and this full package help bring solutions that do not just criminalize delivery workers, but make us safer by understanding the real dangers for all e-bike and scooter operations. We can do that while protecting New Yorkers who depend on these vehicles to make a living or just to get around. I want to thank the advocates, the workers, my colleagues and every New Yorker who continues to inform this work.
