(03:49:18)

Good afternoon. So I just want to put it simply by saying we go where the data is. One of the things that we do is we focus on high-visibility corridors. I mentioned that earlier and I will go through very quickly: 15 corridors identified citywide. The decisions behind the identification process for those corridors are really based on spikes in fatalities, collisions and injuries — where we saw spikes along those criteria. One such corridor I mentioned previously here was Ocean Parkway. Last year, one of the things we do when we see a horrible incident like that horrific collision — three fatalities — we get together, we put all resources at it, right? Again, that corridor gets extra commands. So essentially all 15 corridors touch the entire city. Year to date 2026, fatalities and total collisions are down along the Ocean Parkway corridor, total injuries down, moving violations up and zero movers up. When you look broadly among all 15 corridors, there is a decrease in fatalities — that is always our number one goal, to drive down the numbers along those 15 corridors. It is 11 versus 17, down six fatalities. Again, every life lost is one too many, but there is a decrease in total collisions. Zero movers overall for all 15 corridors year to date: 74,207 moving violations

(03:50:49)

issued along those 15 corridors. So this is the type of attention we put along — so that is one strategy.

(03:50:56)

Another thing also is ghost plate operations. We kind of hit that two ways. Very quickly, we work with law enforcement partners — the Port Authority Police Department, just to name a few. This year, year to date 2026: 29 joint operations. And again, ghost plates — it goes to plates on cars that display fraud, expired, mismatched, no plates, all of that. That world: 418 arrests, 17,000-plus summonses issued, 1,817 vehicles seized and 63 motorcycle and moped seizures. And then also locally, responding to community complaints, we will see apart from obstructed plate parking summonses — they have written 16,236 summonses. This is again by our uniform personnel. This is obstructed plate moving summonses: 20,719 arrests made for temp tag fraud and arrests: 1,833 year to date. So we have that just broken down.

I also wanted to mention the third thing that we see, which helps us move from spot to spot — again, just looking at spikes. One thing we have seen recently is two things: lack of seatbelt compliance. When you see that, it means occupants are being ejected out of vehicles. We will attack that by reaching out to our partners, New York City and New York State. So when you look on the highways you see "buckle up" and that type of awareness, but we have short-term initiatives to address that along certain corridors. Speed is one of the things we will do. In this particular case, after a rash of tragic incidents, speed and seatbelts. As it changes, the micro-mobility world — we mentioned this earlier — point-of-sale initiatives. We started that in 2021 and continue this year. I would say with great success. The work has not ended with identifying additional retailers and we will continue to do so. Again, our enforcement — we have the enforcement numbers. We do target a number of violations, the worst of the worst, and that is reckless driving,

(04:00:41)

In order to correlate how different incentives, or number of trips, or length of trips, or time of trips, or location of trips relate to the safety outcomes, we need that trip-level data. All right. My last set of questions are about our enforcement against retailers. According to previous statements from the Department of Consumer Protection, one issue the agency has encountered regarding enforcement against retailers is that Section 20 of the Administrative Code, which prohibits the sale and rental of uncertified e-bikes and batteries, is too forgiving. This is because when a business first violates such prohibition, the agency cannot actually issue a penalty, as Section 20-610 provides that the first violation carries a civil penalty of $0. Only after catching the business violating 20-610 a second time within two years of the first violation can it fine, and even then the fine is only $250. However, despite these issues, it appears the City's enforcement efforts continue to rely primarily on Section 20-610.

(04:05:14)

Can you explain the issues the agency has faced under Section 20-610 and whether it still feels that its penalty structure, and particularly the inability to fine a business for their first violation, makes it harder to achieve compliance?

(04:05:34)

I think it does, Chair. As a general matter, as a law enforcement agency, I believe strongly in the importance of deterrence. And when you get a first violation that does not deter the worst conduct. I will say that our enforcement is having results. We are seeing more compliance by businesses. A lot of these businesses quickly do come into compliance, and I want to recognize that maybe they were not aware of their legal obligations. But given the very serious issues the Council and our City are confronting around safety, I think we would be very open to a conversation about changing the first-time penalty structure for these laws.

(04:06:13)

Thank you. As you know, the penalty structure under Section 20-762, which contains the City's ban on the sale of motorized scooters, is much less lenient. If it finds a business to be in violation of the motorized scooter ban, a civil penalty of $1,000 must be imposed, which increases to $2,000 for any subsequent violation within one year. However, we see much fewer fines issued for violations of 20-762. This is surprising as it seems that most devices that would be illegal to sell under 20-610 would also be illegal to sell under 20-762.

(04:06:50)

(04:07:10)

On the last part, Chair — yes. When we do an inspection of one of these businesses, either online or in person, we are going to look at violations across the board and issue summonses accordingly. We have issued 50 summonses against illegal motorized scooters over the last nine months of 2026. I think what that number does not capture, though, is the very large number of citations and confiscations that NYPD has issued, which is part of the administration's approach. So most of the enforcement, I would say, Chair, is on the street rather than at the point of sale.

(04:07:45)

So regarding 20-610, you are open to changing the penalty structure? We are, yes. And with respect to 20-762, our expectation is that we will see more enforcement on that statute for the sale of these devices, which we believe can also be equally captured under 20-610.

As I have testified earlier and throughout this hearing, we are never going to have safety on New York City streets if our delivery workers feel they need to choose between their livelihood and their safety — if they feel, as Chair Brewer said, that the companies are imposing unrealistic delivery times on them such that they have to cut corners. Good afternoon, Chair. Good afternoon. I thought it was going to have more people in the panel, but it is all right. I am Antonio Reynoso. Zero of those people have been killed by e-bikes or e-scooters. Generally, e-bikes and scooters and mopeds are responsible for 5% of pedestrian injuries and 5% of pedestrian fatalities. That means 95% of pedestrian injuries and fatalities are caused by cars. Yet here we are having a hearing with 21 Bills aimed at regulating e-bikes and scooters as well as mopeds, which are already illegal. None are about regulating dangerous driving and only two are about infrastructure. Meanwhile, important safety Bills like universal daylighting still have not been passed. When was the last time the City Council had a hearing with 21 Bills aimed at improving safety by curbing dangerous driving?

Some of the Bills being heard today will be helpful. We should focus on regulating businesses that sell e-bikes and companies that incentivize unsafe practices. But instead, too many of the Bills are allowing enforcement that will absolutely be carried out in a discriminatory way that punishes people of color and workers who are just trying to get around the city to do their jobs. Every major city that has tried to pass bike licensing Bills has either repealed it or decided not to try it in the first place, because it has failed so often. It is unwieldy, it is expensive, and it gives police another excuse to target people of color. The idea of getting police to report on targeted traffic enforcement is a joke, and half-stepping measures like writing "look up" on streets just blames pedestrians for getting themselves hit. What we need instead is to end car culture. The Council should support the administration in actually implementing the Streets Plan. Thank you, your time is up. Thank you.

Last year we partnered with DOT on the e-bike trade-in program targeting unsafe batteries. The $2 million program traded in a total of 400 bikes at an approximate cost of $5,000 per bike. To trade in up to 20,000 Class 3 bikes would cost the City a hundred million dollars, assuming it is even feasible to source that many e-bikes and convince delivery workers to switch from their trusted bikes to new ones. We faced a lot of skepticism.

Our proposed alternative is for the Council to fund a transition program that would alter the motors of existing legal Class 3 bikes so they could not go faster than 20 miles per hour, and provide speedometers to delivery workers so they know how fast they are going. This would cost a fraction of the cost of a full bike trade-in program and would allow delivery workers to keep their trusted bikes they know can withstand the rigors of a 12-hour delivery shift. It would also enable the City to fund small businesses and bike shops to provide these alterations. Instead of banning the tools that delivery workers depend on to work, we should focus on the underlying goal: reducing bike speeds. Thank

Workers should also be protected when we choose safety over speed. We are asking the City to listen to delivery workers and bring us to the table. We know the difference between safe and not illegal. We need safety, not punishment.

Good afternoon. Thank you to the chairs for holding this hearing. My name is Michael Sutherland. I am the Senior Policy and Legislative Analyst at Open Plans, an advocacy organization fighting for a more livable city. Micromobility provides an alternative to car trips, which is especially important in the outer boroughs like southern Brooklyn, where I was born and raised.

At Open Plans, we believe three pillars make up effective micromobility policy: point-of-sale regulation, enforcement and infrastructure. We support Int 0950-2026 and Int 0986-2026 to address dangerous devices at the point of sale. We have serious concerns about Preconsidered Int 0244-2026, which would ban the sale and use of Class 3 bikes. We are supportive of a ban on the sale of Class 4 bikes, but a ban on use concerns us, as warnings issued through this bill could create an environment for pretextual stops, resulting in a disproportionate impact on undocumented New Yorkers. We are supportive of the concept of Int 0994-2026, though we have thoughts about the mechanisms of enforcement which we detail further in our written testimony, which we will submit. We support Int 1066-2026, which would add traffic calming measures to bike lanes.

However, we believe that infrastructure is otherwise sorely missing from the conversation at this hearing. We encourage the Council to explore ways we can make use of these bikes more safe and convenient, like wider or protected bike lanes and dedicated space for micromobility on our busiest micromobility corridors, as CM Krishnan was mentioning earlier. We support the concept of Int 0095-2026 — a training program is essential in getting certain devices off the streets without limiting New Yorkers' ability to get around. Lastly, we strongly discourage the Council from hyperfocusing on delivery workers as the primary issue with micromobility. This does a disservice to the delivery workers who provide an essential service to our city and to the task at hand of making our streets safer for all users. To this end, we oppose Res 0043-2026. Our positions on these bills and others in this hearing are expanded further in our written testimony. Thank you for your time.

(05:16:58)

My name is Josh Golden. I am testifying today on behalf of Uber. Uber supports regulations that make our streets safer for all New Yorkers, including delivery workers. Over the past six years, the Council has passed dozens of laws regulating delivery apps. Uber has distributed more than 8,000 helmets and 37,000 safety vests over the past 12 months. App-based delivery workers represent only a piece of device traffic. Citi Bike users take more than a million trips each week, and it is found that 40% of restaurants use their own delivery workers. We need stronger enforcement of the laws already on the books, not simply more regulation of one segment of riders.

Let us be clear: none of these bills will work without enforcement. For example, it is still too easy to purchase and operate unregistered mopeds or devices that lack UL certification. I want to comment specifically on three of the bills before you today. On Int 0994-2026, we strongly oppose the proposed mandate, which we believe is disproportionate. Citi Bike has operated under a City contract for more than a decade without this type of third-party liability requirement, and the bill would require a worker riding a 60-pound bicycle to carry six times the liability coverage required of someone driving a 6,000-pound vehicle. On Int 0989-2026, workers are paid for their delivery time and Uber does not pay them more for riding faster or penalize them for taking longer. If other companies do this, rather than having a study and more data in perpetuity, just ban the practice. On the Preconsidered introduction on tracking workers, we agree with the underlying safety elements of this proposal. We are highly concerned that this legislation is overly prescriptive. Specifically, it risks taking away workers' ability to earn a living by relying on tracking technology that is frequently problematic in dense parts of New York City, where satellite signals bounce, drop and miscalculate speeds among high-rises. Thank you for your time and the opportunity to testify.

The data highlights two things worth paying attention to. One...

(05:19:56)

An increasing share of these fires are non-structural, which means that they are occurring outside of charging context, igniting on the street after overuse, in a trash can, or through improper disposal. A growing number of these fires are caused by batteries reaching the end of their useful life cycle, even when a battery was UL 2271 certified at the point of sale. A UL 2271 certification says that the battery was safe on the day it was sold, but not that it was still safe after months of damage or charging cycles on City streets. The fix is simple: regular inspection, maintenance and safe retirement across the battery's full life cycle, available to every rider and not left to the rider alone. This could be done in-house by fleet operators, trained retailers, through a City-run assessment program, or through a third-party provider, of which battery swapping networks like ours are just one existing option. So we have three specific asks. The first is to amend Local Law 95 of 2025 to mandate ongoing battery life cycle management — the regular inspection, maintenance and timely... Thank you very much.

One of the most important things we can do to accomplish our shared goals for street safety is to be consistent with the accurate definition of an e-bike in New York. An e-bike or high-speed bicycle with electric assist has a motor of less than 750 watts, working pedals and cannot exceed a maximum speed of 25 miles per hour. Many of the electric devices recently involved in traffic fatalities do not fit this definition and are more accurately described as e-mopeds or high-speed electric scooters. They are often confused with bikes or scooters. Conflating those currently illegal devices with bikes or scooters leads us to address perceived rather than actual street safety. And while the greatest threat to cyclists, pedestrians and other motorists — as many have said today — remains cars and trucks, faster, heavier devices like e-mopeds are presenting an increasing danger on our streets.

We have provided detailed written testimony covering each bill, but there are overarching goals that we would like the Council to consider. We should promote cycling, including high-speed bikes for people who choose them. We should eliminate e-mopeds or high-speed scooters from our streets and bike lanes. We should regulate third-party delivery services that encourage unsafe behavior and should not regulate their riders. Licensing or registering bikes is ineffective at promoting safety and reducing the use of e-mopeds. Rules and targets should avoid inequitable outcomes and have clear goals. And perhaps most importantly, we need to improve our street network and street design to support safe micro-mobility and take the onus for street safety off pedestrians and riders. We look forward to continuing to partner with the Council and agencies to make sure the final legislation will truly address street safety while preserving access to micro-mobility as an accessible and affordable option for New Yorkers. Clearly. Thank you. Hello. My name is Anna Humphrey and I am testifying today on behalf of the Center for Independence of the Disabled, New York. We support Int 0950-2026 because establishing a business licensing framework for delivery services is a critical first step toward accountability. For disabled pedestrians, navigating City sidewalks has become increasingly hazardous due to reckless micro-mobility operation and unsafe commercial delivery practices. When reckless or illegal e-bike use leads to serious injuries, app-based delivery companies regularly shirk responsibility, leaving vulnerable victims with trauma and unrecoverable medical expenses. In 2023, three of those deaths involved bike riders, and most of those in 2023 were working for app-based delivery platforms. By setting clear conditions for licensure and establishing enforcement, Int 0950-2026 ensures that wealthy delivery platforms can finally be held equally, legally and financially accountable for the dangerous conditions created by their business models.

We support Int 0389-2026, which mandates that third-party food delivery apps verify that mopeds used by their workers are properly registered before allowing them onto the platform. Unregistered high-speed mopeds frequently travel illegally on sidewalks, pedestrian plazas and crosswalks, presenting a physical threat to pedestrians with disabilities who cannot react or move out of the way quickly. Without proper registration, hit-and-run incidents involving disabled New Yorkers leave victims with zero recourse or ability to identify those responsible. Int 0389-2026 places the burden directly on the corporations profiting from these deliveries, forcing platforms to ensure that every vehicle operating on their behalf follows basic traffic safety laws before hitting our streets. We urge the City Council to implement these safety measures that protect pedestrians with disabilities who want to navigate our streets without fear. Thank you for the opportunity to comment. Most people who are injured in the streets are hit by cars, and we need to do something about that.

When it comes to Uber and delivery apps, I use delivery apps a lot, and when I am hungry I stare at the screen and I see where the driver is. I can track where he has been and where he is going. It tells the driver how to get there, and they are being told to drive the wrong way on streets. I live in Park Slope, land of one-way streets. The shortcuts they are taking — they are being told to go the wrong way. Just order food and see how they travel.

I think we need to educate our citizens of New York City about safety, both pedestrians and cyclists, and we need to look out for each other. We do not need laws that are unenforceable and punitive. We need to know that people are going to be riding bikes, people are going to be walking, and we all need to get along a little better. I hope we can pass laws to make that happen.

(05:39:10)

Thank you. Next.

(05:39:12)

Good afternoon, committee chairs and members of the City Council. My name is Lloyd Thing and I am the Associate State Director of Advocacy for AARP New York. I am here testifying on behalf of our 750,000 members in New York City. AARP New York supports Int 0214-2026 and Int 0787-2026. Safe streets matter enormously to older adults who rely on walking and public transit to reach medical appointments, buy groceries and stay connected to their communities. For an older pedestrian, street design can determine whether crossing feels routine or dangerous. Crossing times, vehicle speeds, visibility and sidewalk conditions all matter. A 2022 study found that older adults, less than 15% of the City's population, accounted for more than 45% of pedestrian fatalities.

Int 0214-2026 takes the broad approach we have long supported through complete streets, designing for pedestrians, cyclists, transit riders, drivers and people using mobility devices. As the e-bike task force does its work, we ask that it explicitly consider the needs of older adults alongside those of people with disabilities, and examine where and why collisions happen so the City can make targeted infrastructure improvements. Int 0787-2026 addresses data and transparency. Reporting on targeted enforcement will show how resources are used and whether strategies actually reduce dangerous behavior, crashes and injuries. Enforcement should focus on behavior that endangers people and it should be judged on evidence. Both bills rest on the same principle: use data, thoughtful design and evidence to make our streets safer. AARP New York urges the Council to pass both bills that would bring us closer to making New York City a true age-friendly city. Thank you.

He did not look like he was delivering anything. I was in shock. Luckily, I did not get hurt and my wheelchair still worked so I could get across the rest of the street to my accessible vehicle.

The rider and his e-bike went flying and he landed in the street. Naturally, we did not exchange insurance information. However, my wheelchair was not steering correctly and would unpredictably veer to the left. It turns out that the e-bike bent the steel fork on my wheelchair and I needed a new fork and two new casters for a total of $945 that I must pay. I have not been able to use my regular wheelchair as I still wait for the parts to arrive. They are supposed to come next week, three months after I was hit.

We are in favor of banning class three bikes. They go up to 28 miles per hour and probably beyond. We insist that all bikers follow the rules of the road and do not go over 20 miles per hour. We are also in favor of technology that makes bikes slow way down on sidewalks. Lastly, we need more education and accountability for e-bikes. Thank you.

(06:30:54)

Next. Hi, my name is Darrell Reardon and I am speaking on behalf of the Delivery Worker and Health Study Team. We actually gave you this paperwork already. The Workers Justice Project team surveyed 561 delivery workers this spring and our just-released report, "Driven by Algorithms," that we handed you is submitted to the Committee along with our testimony. The findings from our study are stark: 53% of workers report a work-related injury — more than five times the national rate. 40% have severe psychological distress, five times the rate among adult New Yorkers. Algorithms are behind the negative health effects. Over 80% say the apps decide their speed, routes and hours, and 52% feared deactivation if they do not deliver fast enough. For 96%, this is their only job, and the threat of deactivation and shift limits pushes workers to take risks that endanger them and the public.

These bills tackle a real problem as e-bikes become a permanent part of our streets, and we support those that improve street design, education and crash data. But as drafted, nine of the bills on today's agenda could have serious unintended consequences. Local Law 34, aimed at preventing unjust deactivations, does not cover temporary lockouts, and it exempts deactivations the law would require. Some of these bills would mandate lockouts and others would make deactivation easier to justify, giving apps the ability to cut out workers and push them to rush even more. Our written testimony details our recommendations. Finally, Local Law 34 should be amended to cover temporary lockouts. Blocking caps and shift limits — safer streets require limiting the apps' power to make workers rush. We urge the Council to aim at the root causes of unsafe streets: the algorithms and business decisions of platform delivery companies. Thank you. Thank you, Erik.

(06:33:09)

Thank you, Majority Leader Abreu and your staff for the opportunity to speak today. My name is Eric McLaurin. I am the Executive Director of Streets Pack. I would like to talk about a four-point framework for improving micro-mobility safety.

Number one: 20 is plenty. That should be the top speed permitted on most City streets. Any powered micro-mobility device capable of speeds exceeding 20 miles per hour should be phased out, should not be operated by clients, and should not be classified or marketed as an e-bike or scooter. Number two: stopping the sale of illegal and noncompliant high-speed devices. The sale of high-speed devices masquerading as legal e-bikes — they do not possess a vehicle identification number necessary for registration — must be banned at point of sale to prevent their use on City streets. Three: holding delivery apps accountable. Companies must end unreasonable delivery expectations and stop incentivizing unsafe behavior. They should be licensed and regulated by the Department of Consumer and Worker Protection. They should be required to provide liability insurance for workers making deliveries on their behalf. As the driven-by-algorithms report released yesterday by Cooney and the Workers Justice Project underscored, delivery workers operate under tremendous pressure. They face outsized physical danger and psychological stress just doing their jobs. Four: increasing safety through better street design. Complete street designs are proven to improve safety for all users.

In order to improve safety around micro-mobility use, the City should prioritize implementation of physically protected bike lanes, speed up the installation of hardened daylighting at intersections, and lower speed limits to 20 miles per hour wherever it is allowed by Sammy's Law, among other measures.

Just really quickly on some of the legislation — I will not take all of them today, but we support Int 0244-2026, CM Hudson's bill, which phases out the sale and use of class three bikes. We support Int 0985-2026, your bill, which creates a trade-in program that would work hand in hand with hers. We support also your bill that would ban the deceptive marketing of high-speed devices as e-bikes. And CM Restler's Int 0960-2026, we also support. Thank you very much for your testimony. Thank you. (07:58:45)

Thanks, Majority Leader. Good evening. My name is Corey Hannigan. I am the Policy Director at Transition Alternatives. I am also a resident of Sunnyside, Queens, and I am a member of the Queens... Today I am actually testifying as an individual. I care deeply about safety and I have spent years advocating alongside families for safe streets both in New Jersey and New York. Legal, low-speed bikes have been a revelation for me and many others in my neighborhood, where it can take twice as long to get to nearby upper Manhattan by transit than by bike, but where steep bridges make conventional bikes physically challenging. My own partner has only started joining me thanks to the availability of electric City Bikes. It has enabled more spontaneous trips together to see friends in nearby neighborhoods and more chance encounters with our neighbors.

It is important to recognize that legal, low-speed bikes have killed none of the nearly 150 New Yorkers killed on our streets this year, and yet much of this nearly 30-bill hearing is focused exclusively on the most... Most people are killed by high-speed motor vehicle drivers. There have been 45 hit-and-runs so far this year, as well as by non-street-legal, high-speed devices that exceed 25 miles an hour, which were involved in both the fatal crashes on the Queensboro Bridge and right here on Chambers Street, and which are often purposefully mislabeled as legal bikes. These are the machines that put us all — pedestrians and bike riders alike — at risk. Licensing low-speed bikes will not work. Restricting access to legal e-bikes will only push more people to choose these faster, heavier options. By making the safer option harder to get, it will disproportionately impact immigrant workers. It will do nothing to meaningfully improve safety. I urge you to focus on the data, focus on infrastructure, and address the real problem. Thank you very much for your time.

(08:10:55)

We know that bike lanes and better infrastructure save lives.

Secondly, size and speed matter. Mass and velocity define the risk categories by which we set rules, and different risk categories should not have identical regulatory regimes. That is why we have separate rules for cars and bikes. It is why the "20 is plenty" rule matters so much, and we should not treat things that go faster than 20 the same as things that go more slowly than 20. That is why rules such as Int 0244-2026 and Int 0988-2026 are so effective and important. We should separate the Class 3 e-bikes and the e-motos from the Class 1 and 2 e-bikes and subject those to a separate regulatory regime.

Finally, for enforcement, the golden rule should be to punch up and not punch down. I am gratified to see that so many of the categories are covering delivery companies, apps and bike stores. They have the knowledge base regarding registration and regarding the technical aspects that a lot of buyers do not, so it is more appropriate to regulate them.
